| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jjcm](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jjcm) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289786) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012)\[–\]<br>Here's a image->html test. Gemini has always swung above its weight class for vision work, so I'm always eager to try it with this.<br>Original images: [https://image.non.io/neonRamenDesigns.webp](https://image.non.io/neonRamenDesigns.webp)<br>Gemini 3.7 build: [https://html.non.io/neonRamenGemini3.7](https://html.non.io/neonRamenGemini3.7)<br>Opus 5 build for comparison: [https://html.non.io/neonRamen](https://html.non.io/neonRamen)<br>Opus is still best in class for this, but it's worth noting how well Gemini 3.7 does vs a more comparable LLM price wise, which is Grok 4.6: [https://html.non.io/neonRamenGrok4.6](https://html.non.io/neonRamenGrok4.6) . I thought Gemini would blow Grok out of the water (it generally has in the past), but Grok has really caught up. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jjcm](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jjcm) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289937) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293916)\[–\]<br>Other thoughts: I really think Google has fallen behind here. Even as a high speed offering (this build took ~7min, which is pretty good!), it wont be able to claim dominance for long with cerebras announcing the Sol preview today: [https://www.cerebras.ai/blog/accelerating-gpt-5-6-sol-ultraf...](https://www.cerebras.ai/blog/accelerating-gpt-5-6-sol-ultrafast-with-openai) .<br>It's not a bad model by any means, but I just don't know what situation I'd reach for 3.7 Flash first for. Google really needs a differentiator, especially given how hard it is to get an API key from them. They can't be high friction and non-pareto. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [krat0sprakhar](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=krat0sprakhar) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290787) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289937) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291029)\[–\]<br>Can you help me understand how it is hard to get an API key from Google? You just head on over to [http://aistudio.google.com/api-keys](http://aistudio.google.com/api-keys) and create a key... not any different from platform.openai.com?<br>Disclaimer: I work in Google so it might be that this link is not publicly well known | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jjcm](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jjcm) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290912) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290787) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294020)\[–\]<br>Disclaimer that I haven't tried this since January, so things may have changed in the last 7mo, but this was my experience at that time: [https://x.com/pwnies/status/2010523020629274723](https://x.com/pwnies/status/2010523020629274723) <br>At a high level though, as a rule of thumb Google assumes that they're serving companies at Google scale first, and at a human scale second. For other companies it's the opposite. Generally what that means is the first experience you get with a Google product will route you through 8 different dashboards to set up ACLs before you've hired your 2nd employee. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [SyneRyder](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=SyneRyder) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291349) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290912) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291703)\[–\]<br>Similar experience here, for what it's worth, though I didn't get as far as you. I basically just stopped and didn't bother - it was easier to go through OpenRouter than spend more energy on it.<br>Also:<br>_\> Google assumes that they're serving companies at Google scale first_<br>So much this. I'm currently grandfathered in until the end of the year on Google's Search API, but the $35,000 they want to continue usage of my < 1000 personal searches per month, not going to happen. It has honestly been easier to use Anthropic to help me build my own search index & crawling infrastructure than deal with Google. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jpadkins](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jpadkins) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49298801) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291349) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294893)\[–\]<br>It's not an assumption that the customer is Google scale that led the $35k monthly price point. It was that you (and many others like you) are breaking the terms of service (which state you are not supposed to store, process or analyze the search results). It was an API built for a different era, that doesn't really exist anymore.<br>\> It has honestly been easier to use Anthropic to help me build my own search index & crawling infrastructure than deal with Google.<br>That's great to hear! FWIW I agree with you that it's harder for independent professionals to get started on Google dev services than others, but the Search API is not developer service and was never intended to be. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [solfox](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=solfox) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291703) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290912) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291349) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291072)\[–\]<br>\> they're serving companies at Google scale first<br>I think that's actually a very interesting insight that would be helpful for PMs on GCloud to take note of. As a single founder, setting up Google Cloud, it's like they start out by assuming you're bigco, forcing (I assume most) of their users into a arduous process of _removing_ components they don't need.<br>Google AI Studio is one of Google's solutions to this problem, but in typical Google fashion, it's bolted-on without any clear connection in the ecosystem. If you're also using GCloud, it's hard to remember it's even _there_.<br>OpenAI's platform, by contrast, is streamlined, easy to use. With Google, I feel like I need to wade through the documentation first before even using the darn thing. | |

| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [ethbr1](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=ethbr1) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49296983) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291703) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49296189) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292423)\[–\]<br>\> _As a single founder, setting up Google Cloud, it's like they start out by assuming you're bigco_ <br>My impression of anything enterprise (big or small) related to Google is that they just don't care / value solving it.<br>Which is sad, because "How does an enterprise customer pay for X?" is a non-trivial and incredibly important UX problem.<br>They have great technical solutions, but these are hamstrung by a frankly amateur understanding of how companies (startups to Fortune 500s) need to sign up, pay for, and track things.<br>From an outside perspective, one of the biggest gaps seems to be that internal Google product teams don't have to dogfood the full GCP et al. project/org experience. They get prebuilt billing structures (or just get to avoid them with internal cross-billing).<br>\-\-\-<br>If I could waive a magic wand, Google would appoint an "Enterprise Czar", reporting directly to Pichai, who is a non-technical, retired founder / CEO.<br>That person would have one job: try to sign up, run (their team), and budget strategic Google initiatives (like AI) as a blind external party.<br>They would then deliver continuous reports to Pichai about how hard / easy this is.<br>Because potential Google customers don't give a shit if it's this internal team or that internal team's responsibility for integrating New Product X into GCP billing.<br>They care that the experience is terrible, filled with friction, and often flat out doesn't work. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jppittma](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jppittma) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295383) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294478) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294814)\[–\]<br>I think that argument should stop with advocacy for law. I have no problem with this when it's means playing optimally within the current laws, things like hiring world class tax attorneys. But if using your immense wealth to ensure the rules that everyone plays by favor you specifically, instead of the country/world, It feels like this is closer to bribing a judge than hiring a tax attorney. | |

| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [fauigerzigerk](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=fauigerzigerk) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295836) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49295383) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294814)\[–\]<br>I would normally agree with that, but in this particular case "everyone" is just over 200 individuals. And he could be opposing it on principle as well.<br>A one-off retroactive wealth tax is a rather questionable form of taxation even if (like myself) you are in favour of looking for ways to tax the super rich more effectively.<br>When I read about these one-off wealth taxes I find one thing really astonishing. The argument against wealth taxes in general is that people would change their behaviour, which would reduce the tax take over time. That's certainly true.<br>But then left wing economists believe that imposing it retroactively and calling it a "one-off" does not change people's behaviour. Technically, it cannot change their behaviour with respect to this specific taxation event.<br>But I find it utterly naive to believe that this sort of hit and run taxation will not change people's behaviour in other ways that would reduce tax revenues.<br>No one in their right mind would ever believe that a tax that brings in $100bn over 5 years will not have to be replaced by some other tax paid by the same group or by the somewhat less well off who decided to stay. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [claytongulick](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=claytongulick) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293833) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292021) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293706)\[–\]<br>This happened to me once when I was setting up some virtual servers for a startup.<br>I wanted a typical dev/qa/prod with medium specced boxes.<br>I was denied for quota, with an esoteric process for review.<br>I'd just made a case for deploying to GCP over AWD so got a bit of egg on my face. Went over and had it done on AWS in a few minutes.<br>A couple days later, the Google product team contacted me. I told them what happened.<br>It got escalated, and I ended up on a call with like 5 or 6 people from Google, some very senior. I told them what happened.<br>They made very concerned sounding noises and told me how this was a product failure on their part, how they'd get it corrected, etc... and they'd fixed my account so I could now make the machines. Of course, I was already deployed to AWS at that point.<br>That company grew and ended up with a pretty big cloud spend eventually. Google totally missed it.<br>I was at a new startup a few years later and decided to deploy to GCP.<br>Denied for quota. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [drschwabe](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=drschwabe) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293706) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290912) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292021) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49295242)\[–\]<br>Literally just built a custom Adsense dashboard based on _not_ integrating with Google's APIs. Every couple days I export the 2 reports I need from their UI and save them to a folder; it's automated from there and integrates with my clients' website - thats good enough - even if its not real time its a small price to pay for not having to navigate (and maintain) Google's API madness. Like you said 8 different dashboards before you get what you need (and frontier LLMs cant help here), and even then you are forced to build some elaborate Oauth app instead of just getting a simple API key that you can paste in a .env file | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [1bm](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=1bm) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290946) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290787) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294020) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291826)\[–\]<br>You need to create a Google Cloud project to create an api key and when you try to create one you very often get error messages like:<br>“Failed to create project, The request is suspicious. You can then navigate multiple screens in GCP to make it work but it’s a hassle compared to any other provider (OAI/Ant/OpenRouter or any of the Chinese labs). | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [qlte](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=qlte) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291156) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290946) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291826)\[–\]<br>I didn't have that issue back when I originally created my API keys a couple years ago. Just out of curiosity I switched to a different Google account that had never interacted with AI Studio and never used Google Cloud console.<br>It was literally two clicks, and didn't even leave the page: the dialog asked to create a project and type in a name, I did that, clicked submit and then it was selected as the default project. One more click and I had the free tier API key.<br>Not saying you didn't have that experience at the time, but personally I have had zero issues with AI Studio and consider it the most dead simple/fastest dev dashboard to get started compared to the others like OpenAI/Anthropic (thanks to Google's free tier that lets you skip billing setup annoyances just to play around with Gemini).<br>Despite what HN threads (that are also frequently confused and talking about GCP instead) portray as universal/widespread issues or the process being complex and time consuming somehow. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [the\_bigfatpanda](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=the_bigfatpanda) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49297087) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290787) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290979) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49301589)\[–\]<br>As someone who has been running Gemini models in production for a year, recently (last 2 months), I have been actively moving away from it.<br>The primary reason for me has been that Google autonomously decides to downgrade usage tiers and then upgrade them again - and does this incorrectly.<br>Over the last week itself, in the span of two days, our account for first downgraded and then upgraded. This is despite matching the criteria to remain at the tier we operate at throughout.<br>Google Support (when you finally get to a human) has accepted that these are potentially bugs, but the first time it happened, we were rate limited so severely for ~4 hours that I find it really difficult to continue trusting Google. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [ycui7](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=ycui7) [43 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49356061) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290451) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292228)\[–\]<br>Cerebras is an uncut whole wafer. Each wafer gets you 44GB SRAM (not a typo, SRAM, not DRAM/VRAM). A few years ago, leading process node wafer from tsmc is $20K/ea without guarantee on yield.<br>A single full wafer likely can run qwen3.6-27b alone. But won't be enough to run bigger models, which are pretty much all popular models. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jaggederest](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jaggederest) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49292228) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290451) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49356061) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290848)\[–\]<br>Is it? I think waferscale might actually be cheaper per-token, it's just so many more tokens, and of course right now it's not a full buildout so the availability is limited as well. I'd imagine they'll be migrating to whichever inference method is least expensive, and I expect asics to be the ultimate answer. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [sigmoid10](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=sigmoid10) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290848) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290451) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292228) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290414)\[–\]<br>Moving from either frontier intelligence _or_ frontier latency to a single model that does _both at the same time_ is potentially a game changer in certain industries. I can easily see e.g. hedge funds dropping tons of money on this, because it means they can now do the same thing as their competitors, but much faster. That's basically a license to print money. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [navorad772](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=navorad772) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49292092) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290848) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291949)\[–\]<br>I am not sure this is the way to make AI more cost effective for such customers. If they are able to tweak any model for their use case it would be way more reliable and also way cheaper. In my opinion generic LLMs in the future will be just for attention economy or maybe government contracts. Everyone else will be running fine tuned free weight models or licenced closed source models (self hosted or managed). | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jmalicki](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jmalicki) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293110) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291949) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292293)\[–\]<br>It's not like there are only two buckets:<br>1\. HFT doing ass-simple arbitrage where only latency matters<br>2\. More sophisticated slower trading taking in deeper signals<br>Those are two points along a continuum. If you are reacting to an earnings announcement by having an LLM read the earnings release and listen to the call, getting the results a few seconds earlier lets you get your trade in a few seconds earlier. Just because "not HFT" doesn't mean "completely latency insensitive". | |

Except that ultrafast delivers this level of intelligence an order of magnitude faster. So if your competitors automatically react to news articles or financial statements with a certain level of comprehension within minutes, you can now do so in seconds. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [cameronh90](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=cameronh90) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49292293) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291949) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293110) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290414)\[–\]<br>There's a lot of trading that isn't proper "HFT", but where speed and latency still matter. Often you'll find this employed more as slippage reduction - i.e you're going to make the trade either way, but making it faster saves you a few bps.<br>I'm not sure what event-based traders are doing now, but back in the day NLP sentiment analysis was all the rage, so I'm assuming they've now incorporated LLMs too. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [Barbing](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=Barbing) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295002) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293916) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49298416)\[–\]<br>Sigh. You might be interested in this piece that came to mind from Doctorow:<br>```<br>  “Let me explain: on average, illustrators don't make any money. They are already one of the most immiserated, precarized groups of workers out there. They suffer from a pathology called "vocational awe." That's a term coined by the librarian Fobazi Ettarh, and it refers to workers who are vulnerable to workplace exploitation because they actually care about their jobs – nurses, librarians, teachers, and artists.<br>  If AI image generators put every illustrator working today out of a job, the resulting wage-bill savings would be undetectable as a proportion of all the costs associated with training and operating image-generators. The total wage bill for commercial illustrators is less than the kombucha bill for the company cafeteria at just one of Open AI's campuses.<br>  The purpose of AI art – and the story of AI art as a death-knell for artists – is to convince the broad public that AI is amazing and will do amazing things. It's to create buzz. Which is not to say that it's not disgusting that former OpenAI CTO Mira Murati told a conference audience that "some creative jobs shouldn't have been there in the first place," and that it's not especially disgusting that she and her colleagues boast about using the work of artists to ruin those artists' livelihoods.”<br>```<br>[https://pluralistic.net/2025/12/05/pop-that-bubble/](https://pluralistic.net/2025/12/05/pop-that-bubble/) <br>OK so after all that, THIS (bad foam party posters) is what we get! | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [paulluuk](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=paulluuk) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49296256) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49295002) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49298416)\[–\]<br>I like this concept of "vocational awe", and I do think it's why, for example, there is so much sexual and financial abuse in the movie, music and game industry: those people are willing to be treated poorly so they can work their craft - do the thing they love. Basically they are trading away good working conditions in exchange for actually doing work you like or find meaningful, the opposite of someone who does something they hate or find meaningless but pays a high salary and where you are treated really well.<br>I don't know how well this actually translates to AI, though. We all understand that AI art has a pretty low quality, but sometimes low quality is enough. If I want an image for my D&D character that only I and my DM will likely ever see, I am fine with a 7 cent AI-generated image, but I'm not willing to pay 150$ for an artist to do it - not that I don't value their time, I just don't value the image that much. Before AI this was the same, I'd just have used an image from Pinterest and thought "Well, this isn't exactly a good match, but I can't find anything better".<br>But I assume real illustrators do things like illustrations for children's books? I'd like to believe that those are still done by actual people, not AI. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [DrewADesign](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=DrewADesign) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293981) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292420) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289985)\[–\]<br>Unfortunately, as soon as they can’t find work, everybody interested in the more easily automated dev work will suddenly become very interested in up-skilling into all _other_ kinds of dev work. So then you have an excess supply, which means little job security and littler salaries. Developers were in the cool kid club in SV because it was more painful to fill dev roles than to treat developers with kid gloves. Without high labor demand, there is no leverage for developers. Increasingly, management has the leverage. Oh well. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [xyzsparetimexyz](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=xyzsparetimexyz) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291511) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292172) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012)\[–\]<br>This has so much less character than the pelican smdh..<br>Plus is the ramen in HK even any good? | |

| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jjcm](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jjcm) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49292316) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291511) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012)\[–\]<br>I think they're both testing very different things. The pelican test is testing if a LLM can come up with visuals on its own via writing bezier curves directly.<br>This is testing if it can match visuals that have already been established, and represent them with all the tools available to a web developer. The ramen example was chosen in particular because there are a lot of things that aren't easy to do with CSS, and require creative strategies: 45deg button cuts, angular repeating pattern elements, blending of raster art and svgs, microglyphs, low contrast subtle elements, etc.<br>Don't ask yourself whether it's a good design, as yourself whether it's a good test. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [simonw](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=simonw) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290012) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289786) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829)\[–\]<br>The "introductory pricing" for this 3.7 Flash model is really weird.<br>It's scheduled to double in price on December 31, 2026, but who would anticipate still using this model five months from now? Especially since 3.6 Flash came out just three weeks ago!<br>My first effort with default thinking level produced an ambitious pelican, let down by a flawed bicycle: [https://tools.simonwillison.net/markdown-svg-renderer#url=ht...](https://tools.simonwillison.net/markdown-svg-renderer#url=https%3A%2F%2Fgist.github.com%2Fsimonw%2F8df5fd1b58f128cff27f57f0f959e169)<br>Then I ran it on high, medium and low thinking levels (oddly minimal is no longer an option, which WAS an option for 3.5 and 3.6) and got a pretty excellent pelican for the first two:<br>[https://tools.simonwillison.net/markdown-svg-renderer.html#u...](https://tools.simonwillison.net/markdown-svg-renderer.html#url=https%3A%2F%2Fgist.github.com%2Fsimonw%2F6779a22d5e7bb6bdf29936f1600a5259)<br>UPDATE: That was in Safari, but as pointed out in the replies here the pelicans do NOT render well in Firefox or Chrome! Best guess is that's because of this invalid filter in the SVG:<br>```<br>  <filter id="shadow" x="-10%" y="-10%" width="130%" height="130%"></filter><br>```<br>Filters are meant to contain additional elements, not be empty: [https://drafts.csswg.org/filter-effects/#FilterElement](https://drafts.csswg.org/filter-effects/#FilterElement) \- so maybe Chrome and Firefox remove the element that references the broken filter but Safari doesn't? | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [abtinf](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=abtinf) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290567) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291197)\[–\]<br>\> got a pretty excellent pelican for the first two<br>This suggests you primarily use Safari.<br>While the bike renders, the pelican doesn’t in Chrome and Firefox.<br>Probably one of the more serious defects I’ve seen with the pelican. It’s one thing when animated SVGs have bugs, but another when plain ones do. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [wongarsu](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=wongarsu) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291197) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290567) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290761)\[–\]<br>At $work we still have some places using Opus 4.5. Even places using Qwen 2.5-VL, which is now 18 months old. It works, and upgrading is work (we'd have to validate the new model performs comparable in all the corner cases that currently work just fine)<br>Those kind of workloads would be hit by an end of introductory pricing. And it's exactly the kind of cases that are not very price sensitive. Where we are price sensitive we track new model releases closely, where we aren't other issues get priority as long as llm performance is good enough | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [GodelNumbering](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=GodelNumbering) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290761) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291197) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292111)\[–\]<br>I think introductory here means more or less permanent but they can't publicly admit there are no takers at a higher price.<br>Anthropic for instance announced a couple of days ago that they are making Sonnet's 'introductory pricing' permanent [https://xcancel.com/claudeai/status/2086891169217122586](https://xcancel.com/claudeai/status/2086891169217122586) | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [MikhailTal](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=MikhailTal) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291867) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290761) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292111)\[–\]<br>When a company gives away service a heavily subsidized service as a promo, the full cost of serving it (compute) can get classified as sales and marketing instead of just cost of revenue, which makes your gross margin look better! | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [asdfman123](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=asdfman123) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49292111) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290761) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294146)\[–\]<br>Maybe it's a way to kick people off the old models. "If you still want to keep using the old model you can, but you'd better pay a premium."<br>I work for Google and their free, internal Gemini API isn't quite as graceful. They once turned down a model arbitrarily and it broke our tests. I had to scramble to fix it, then build warning systems for the turndown as well as a special validator to make sure any upgrades make the same determinations. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [Alifatisk](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=Alifatisk) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289829) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290012) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289334)\[–\]<br>Ever since the insane discount with GPT-5.6 Luna, not much excites me anymore. I mean just look at the benchmarks, even though Gemini 3.7 Flash performs well on the DeepSWE 1.1, Luna (Max) still performs way better. I personally have stuck to Luna (Xhigh) because its been more than enough and does not bloat up the context window too fast with reasoning tokens.<br>[https://deepswe.datacurve.ai](https://deepswe.datacurve.ai/)<br>\> Starting January 1, 2027, $1.50/1M input tokens and $7.50/1M output tokens will apply.<br>Compare this to Luna which is at $0.2/1M input ($0.02 cached) and $1.2/1M output.<br>[https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/gpt-5.6-luna](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/gpt-5.6-luna) | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [z\_rho\_one](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=z_rho_one) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290516) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290056)\[–\]<br>GPT-5.6 Luna is an insanely powerful model for its price. It's been great for coding workflows where I guide the LLM's hand step by step. It's also insane to see my weekly limit drop by than 2% after an hour of coding ever since the discount.<br>However, I've noticed 2 drawbacks with Luna. Context rot is much more palpable than Terra and Sol. It tends to get confused and go into rabbit holes when it's context gets filled up. In addition, when instructions are vague, it performs poorly and tends to write way to more code than necessary, but that is to be expected of smaller models. In all, for clearly defined, bite-sized coding tasks, Luna's price-to-performance has been insane. It might have very well commanded the price tag of Sol if it came out just a year ago. | |

Closer to Gemini than Terra... | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [pimeys](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=pimeys) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295828) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293174) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49296415)\[–\]<br>Yes. It works very well for simple tasks. When I know the context grows over 200k, I implement with Kimi.<br>We run an agent company and we do a bunch of different things with agents. Where we used Gemini before Deepseek v4 Flash is taking the lead on price. It's like 5x cheaper than 3.6 and well 2.5x cheaper than 3.7 "introductory price". Comparable quality. | |

| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [homebessguy](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=homebessguy) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49296415) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293174) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49295828) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290056)\[–\]<br>Interesting - how are you interacting and orchestrating this? | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [pimeys](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=pimeys) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49298400) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49296415) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290056)\[–\]<br>Not the parent, but:<br>[https://omp.sh/](https://omp.sh/)<br>You define roles for different agents like this:<br>```<br>  modelRoles: <br>    task: fireworks/kimi-k3-fast:high<br>    plan: fireworks/kimi-k3-fast:max<br>    slow: fireworks/kimi-k3-fast:max<br>    smol: fireworks/deepseek-v4-flash-0731:low<br>    tiny: fireworks/gpt-oss-20b<br>    vision: fireworks/qwen3.7-plus:high<br>    designer: fireworks/qwen3.7-plus:high<br>    advisor: openai-codex/gpt-5.6-sol:high<br>    main_worker: fireworks/kimi-k3-fast:high<br>    fast_worker: fireworks/deepseek-v4-flash-0731:low<br>    vision_worker: fireworks/qwen3.7-plus:high<br>    research_worker: fireworks/glm-5.2:medium<br>    code_worker: fireworks/kimi-k2.7-code-fast:high<br>    review_worker: anthropic/claude-fable-5:high<br>    security_review_worker: fireworks/kimi-k3-fast:max<br>    minimal_worker: fireworks/gpt-oss-20b<br>    default: fireworks/kimi-k3-fast<br>  task: <br>    agentModelOverrides: <br>      task: "@main_worker"<br>      sonic: "@fast_worker"<br>      scout: "@fast_worker"<br>      designer: "@vision_worker"<br>      librarian: "@research_worker"<br>      reviewer: "@review_worker"<br>      security-reviewer: "@security_review_worker"<br>```<br>Then you first say /plan and use some big model like K3. Finally the harness shows you a markdown you approve, and in approval you switch to a smaller model and reset the context. The smaller model gets the full plan and starts working on it. When done, you say /review and it spawns N review agents and returns the change suggestions. And you iterate on that. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [dannyw](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=dannyw) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293234) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293056) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49305596)\[–\]<br>I really, really like DSv4 Flash because you see the full, real thinking text. That’s been so useful for helping steer the model; as well as seeing its thoughts and correcting any errors, or expanding on it. It’s so difficult for me to use closed models with no or summarised thinking now — it feels so painful and gimped.<br>You don’t know what you’re missing until you’ve seen it. For me it’s almost like going from standard def to HD for the first time.<br>(This applies to other open models too — Kimi K3 in real world feels below Opus 5 in terms of raw intelligence, but significantly above Opus 5 in usability and personality. And no silly refusals — the model feels like it’s working for me; not working for Anthropic who’s always holding a leash over the model while I pay for it). | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [estebarb](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=estebarb) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49305596) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293056) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293234) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290165)\[–\]<br>I prefer V4 flash, but Luna is ok and included in the OpenAI plan I'm already paying, so... that is the reason I use it.<br>I gave DeepSeek $50 around june, and I haven't been able to exhaust them yet. The model is super cheap and more than enough for my needs.<br>I'm my opinion, Flash V4 is less pedantic than OpenAI models, less prone to unsolicited prescriptions and less prone to "helpfully" reinterpreting my instructions (wrongly, of course). | |

| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [isamu\_2000](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=isamu_2000) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290165) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290056) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290412)\[–\]<br>luna is the first model that has outdone gpt-5-mini on the pareto frontier for some of my high value, cost sensitive ai product workflows. it's both cheaper (by about 60% in real world use) and higher quality based on my test harnesses. I was really worried that costs would go up since there wasn't a replacement as of a few weeks ago and gpt-5-mini is scheduled to be sunset toward the end of the year. So long as they don't randomly sunset this model anytime soon, that worry has now subsided. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [EB66](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=EB66) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295988) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290165) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290412)\[–\]<br>That's interesting, we've had the same results as you at my company. gpt-5-mini was the clear pareto frontier leader for our in-house LLM benchmarks -- benchmarks that we built and tailored to our specific use cases. Then gpt-5.6-luna came along with the price cuts and immediately supplanted gpt-5-mini.<br>I always thought it was a little odd that gpt-5-mini was the leader for so long when more popular benchmarks placed gpt-5-mini further down the roster, but it seems you had the same result too. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [mikepurvis](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=mikepurvis) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290412) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290165) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290213)\[–\]<br>I'm curious how much people are manually curating context these days; I'm increasingly feeling for myself that it being auto-managed inside a front-end like claude code is not ideal, and I'd rather have more control over what exact files and pieces of discovery go into a particular prompt, and the ability to more easily "fork" a session and ask asides or make notes/todos in a way that doesn't disrupt or confuse a more focused task going on.<br>I don't think I want a gastown-style "just yolo everything" approach, in fact I really want _more_ control over how decisions are made and with what info. Does this exist? | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [Alifatisk](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=Alifatisk) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290584) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290412) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290213)\[–\]<br>I am not sure this enlightens you with anything but I have a TODO.md file with three headlines. Todo, Doing and Done. The agent is aware of it and knows on which task we are on.<br>On complete, it moves the user story from Doing to Done. I also have a MEMORY.md file that the agent read and writes in the beginning of a new conversation and at the end of our conversation to update stale information. These files are referred to every time I start a new conversation.<br>Regarding forking, I know Codex has such button underneath each message that lets you fork the whole conversation. I usually do that when I want to sidetrack and discuss something.<br>I use no SKILLS or commands like /goal. I’ve come a long way with just prompts and markdown files. Its all a different way of encapsulating instructions anyways. | |

| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jolux](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jolux) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290524) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290286) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290473)\[–\]<br>what do you mean by reset at every turn? context stays until compaction. if you remove the reasoning tokens after every turn you will be constantly blowing cache which is far worse than filling up context. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [tym0](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=tym0) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291369) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290524) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290473)\[–\]<br>That's not my understanding of how most agents work. This is what a chain of request/response looks like:<br>```<br>  Your Prompt 1: Prompt Content 1 -> cache-1<br>  LLM Response 1: <Thinking>Thinking Content 1</Thinking> Response Content 1<br>  Your Prompt 2 (client side): prompt-1 + response-without-thinking-1 + Prompt Content 2<br>  Your Prompt 2 (server side): cache-1  + response-without-thinking-1 + Prompt Content 2 -> cache-2<br>  LLM Response 2: <Thinking>Thinking Content 2</Thinking> Response Content 2<br>  Etc...<br>```<br>So reasoning gets dropped from context and you still get cache from the accumulating requests.<br>Edit:<br>I've realised I was incorrect, the thinking doesn't get passed back and forth but the latent snapshot does which result in using memory just the same. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [mike\_hearn](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=mike_hearn) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295813) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291369) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290473)\[–\]<br>Modern protocols loop back the reasoning tokens in raw textual form via an encrypted parameter. You can't see them (modulo the recent attack), but you do resubmit them. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [tym0](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=tym0) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295902) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49295813) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290473)\[–\]<br>Yeah I've done more research and that's what I meant in the edit you replied to.<br>But that's not the full reasoning token context, just a snapshot of the latent state at the end of it, no?<br>Have a look at the gemini ones they're pretty small. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [mike\_hearn](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=mike_hearn) [46 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49311526) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49295902) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290473)\[–\]<br>There was an attack that convinced models to leak the contents of the reasoning tokens, and it came back as text that matched the length of the encrypted data very well. So it's probably still tokens.<br>Looping back latent state isn't that easy. The hidden data is the entire contents of the KV cache which can be massive. I don't think any provider is trying to loop the KV cache through the client, and neural compressions of the reasoning would be lossy / an advanced technique that is still firmly in the realm of research papers, as I understand. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [Alifatisk](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=Alifatisk) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290473) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290286) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290524) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289334)\[–\]<br>Does it reset at every turn? From my experience in Codex for example, Luna (Max) fills the 256k token window relatively quick. The only thing lowering the context window again is the compaction. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [tym0](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=tym0) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291751) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291568) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289334)\[–\]<br>Sorry, I've realised I was only partially correct.<br>Gemini\[0\] for example passes along a snapshot of the reasoning state but it's not the equivalent to keeping all the reasoning tokens in the context.<br>\[0\] [https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/thinking#signatures](https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/thinking#signatures)<br>Edit: Apparently it does take the same space in the LLM latent space so I was wrong. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [sfink](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=sfink) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293411) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289829) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291751) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289334)\[–\]<br>Yeah, I remember reading about Anthropic's experiments with dropping out different things, and dropping the thinking is ok for compaction but pretty awful while it still fits in the context window. I could imagine doing it adaptively -- take a snapshot before the thinking, have a lesser model detect if there's a lot of spinning going on and reset to snapshot + result if so, otherwise accumulate. It'd also be interesting to have a lesser model rewrite thinking to be more streamlined (let's pretend the AI went directly down the right path on the first try). But it may be a disaster anyway -- isn't the thinking section something like a 1d best-logit slice of something like a 2d process, where the real processing could easily be happening under the visible surface?<br>I'm not sure if that's right. I only just recently learned that the "snapshots" (aka cached tokens) necessarily contain the entire context history, not just an image of a "state as of the final token" (well, it is the state as of the final token, but that state contains the whole history). So I'm not confident of my grasp of the structures here. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [WarmWash](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=WarmWash) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49298797) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289334) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294758) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289761)\[–\]<br>5.6 Sol was confident that I had a mite problem, as evidenced by the yellow spots on the leaves. I flipped over the leaves and saw a few small bugs, and relayed this to 5.6 who said that they can be difficult to see since they are so small. The remedy was to blast the underside of all the leaves to wash off all the mites, which I diligently did.<br>However afterwards, still feeling odd that I didn't see many bugs, I double checked with Gemini who is my typical standby for vision tasks. Gemini pointed out that its actually the fungus Corynespora cassiicola...which spreads by water splashing, and can live in your bed for years once it gets into the soil.<br>So now having two answers I need to research and check myself, and sure enough, it was obvious match for the fungus and didn't look much at all like the mite damage.<br>There is another story a few days later of 5.6 looking at my heatpump install and telling me it was probably borked and to call an expert. Gemini told me to just open a valve and it was good.<br>I don't really trust any other LLM besides gemini for vision stuff. It also tracks as google is the only lab still pursuing vision related tasks. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [markasoftware](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=markasoftware) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289650) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289326) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289461) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290739)\[–\]<br>Sol high is almost the same speed if you take into account drastically lower token use. Look at the artificial analysis speed vs token use. Gemini is 7x faster but 5x more tokens. And that's with Sol high being a substantially better model.<br>Edit: and Sol medium actually has the same AA intelligence score as Gemini 3.7, and has >7x fewer tokens, actually making it faster | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [sid\_talks](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=sid_talks) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290739) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289326) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289296)\[–\]<br>The Gemini Flash models makes perfect sense to me coming from a company like Google. Google AI Mode for search is a product I really find useful. It makes sense that Google focused on smaller, faster yet smart enough models that wouldn't break your bank on inference. It plays well into their product ecosystem.<br>Google AI Mode consistently gets me consistently good results and good speeds. It really changes what "googling" is for me. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [gazebo2](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=gazebo2) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49292506) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290739) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290959)\[–\]<br>I similarly have a weird affinity for Gemini that I can't really articulate. I used Gemini's free chat and found it great for exploring technical topics (and random one-off general walking-around-questions) and appreciated its speed, tone and accuracy. I spent a month playing with Gemini CLI / Antigravity and found it also an effective coding agent, at least for my workflow (entirely in the loop development and review). I also was really surprised that I could just paste it images of a project I was working on and have it immediately understand what it was looking at -- which I've come to learn is considered a unique strong point for Gemini. I've been playing with GPT5.6 for about a month and it's definitely powerful but I honestly think I'll go back to Gemini. There's something kind of charming about working with an AI that not only is particularly good at web search and information gathering, but also one that doesn't feel like some superhuman overengineering freak when it comes to code. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [abixb](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=abixb) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49294315) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290739) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292506) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293663)\[–\]<br>Well, Google is probably the only one among frontier model providers in the US that doesn't have a massive financial pressure to deliver business results ASAP (and this focus on agent coding and long-running agentic tasks), so Google is able to focus more on encoding deep scientific, cultural and historical knowledge to its systems. I'd assume DeepMind's focus on the scientific core also played a role in the tone and approach Gemini models take for explanations and Q&As.<br>I find that GPT models and Claude tend to talk in strong slangs and in-group jargon, but love Gemini's massive general knowledge corpus — reminds me of Richard Feynman from his lectures. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [Topfi](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=Topfi) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289242) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289194) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293462)\[–\]<br>\> What's new in Gemini 3.7 Flash \[0\]<br>\> Coding and agentic tasks: Significantly higher quality on real-world software engineering and agentic benchmarks, improving issue resolution and reducing failed agent loops.<br>\> Web development and stronger design parity: Generates higher-fidelity desktop and web application code directly from design mocks, with strong gains in design adherence and in auditing existing codebases against mocks to verify 1:1 design parity.<br>\> Promotional pricing: Gemini 3.7 Flash will be available at an introductory price of $0.75/1M input tokens and $3.75/1M output tokens. We’re also applying this new rate to 3.6 Flash. Introductory pricing expires on December 31, 2026; after, $1.50/1M input tokens and $7.50/1M output tokens will apply.<br>Still no sign of 3.5 Pro. Will have to test it, low expectations given every other model from the Gemini 3 lineage, but one can hope. Just struggle to understand the promotional pricing being temporary for four months. Given this industry, I'd be hard pressed if 3.7 Flash was still in use by end of year, so why not make it the official pricing?<br>\[0\] [https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/latest-model](https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/latest-model) | |

| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [mattlondon](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=mattlondon) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289513) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289359) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289495)\[–\]<br>I have read that "pro"/"opus"/etc models can actually be _worse_ for everyday coding as they reason "too deeply" and turn over too many stones over-thinking the problem and potentially getting distracted.<br>This feels absurd to me (my gut is "I want the SMARTEST model I can get!!"), but often I find that my experience of using a flash/sonnet model for every-day workhorse coding they _are_ better.<br>Its not the same thing, but when I think of that I am reminded of working with some engineers in the past who are incredibly smart and have PhDs (or to put it another way, over-qualified) and they were crap engineers because they'd just not be able to focus on the task and ONLY the task at hand and would get easily distracted by the "why" or "more interesting" things when I just asked them to fix a simple bug or whatever. Again, its not the same thing at all, but it certainly comes to mind when I think of this or experience a pro/opus model suggesting we make huge refactors when a tactical fix is all that is required etc.<br>Of course, the opus-sized models are great when it comes to huge comprehension/research/debugging efforts where the deeper reasoning is actually useful. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [blfr](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=blfr) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289877) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289359) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289513) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289879)\[–\]<br>I find Fable completely unbeatable for anything code-related. It's the only frontier model that seems to come with sane defaults.<br>If it implements something simple like a file export, it just knows that the file should have a meaningful name. Vibecoded feature beats most software's lazy "untitled.png".<br>So, yes, I want the smartest model even for simple stuff. Maybe especially for simple stuff because the tokens burned will be trivial so the cost doesn't give lower models a comparative advantage. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [CoolestBeans](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=CoolestBeans) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289556) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289359) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289495) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289806)\[–\]<br>Probably both. Having a strong frontier model is necessary not just for the model itself but because it provides a halo effect for your entire line. So if Google could deliver a pro model they would. But I also think Google is targeting the wider market and not picking verticals like Anthropic does. A good enough model is good enough for most generalist tasks, and being fast and cheap is more important to less sophisticated users. Also can't forget Google is at every level of the AI vertical. They're not losing sleep because they're not competitive at the one level in which open weight models come out with the quickness. It reflects poorly on them, and from a marketing perspective its not good but in some ways its actually the least valuable place to be. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [customguy](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=customguy) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49292924) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289141) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292035)\[–\]<br>Was excited to try it, since I've been use 3.6 Flash in the last few days to make simply experiments/prototypes. My loop is writing a prompt, maybe adding a screenshot of the closest to what I want I have so far, then based on the result I modify/extend the prompt, maybe use another screenshot.<br>Well, I think I'll stick to 3.6 for now. Based in a very scientific sample size of exactly one attempt each: [https://imgur.com/a/fDOkBDm](https://imgur.com/a/fDOkBDm)<br>Both got the same prompt and example screenshot. I mean, they both suck but that's normal this early in, but the 3.6 version (first screenshot) actually changes the displayed threads depending on selected categories, and the messages of whatever selected thread, as obviously described in the prompt. You might say it more or less does what it should. Both versions have an ugly flash/jerk in the category pane when selecting/deselecting a category, so that's a wash.<br>The 3.7 version doesn't work at all, i.e. it always shows all threads, and no messages for any of them. I can post new messages in threads but they don't show up, and it doesn't even increase the message counter for the thread. I guess it's a matter of taste but I don't like the look either, while 3.6 actually is in the spirit of the screenshot I added the prompt, using 11k of CSS versus 3.7's 16k. The code is also less, and the backend split into 3 files (instead of just 2 as 3.7 did it), so assuming it sucks in either case, it'll be easier to read and massage.<br>edit: geez, 3.6 even properly fades/disables the "new message" button when no thread is selected, 3.7 didn't bother which is smart since everything else is broken anyway. Maybe it's better at _really complex_ things, but for simple things, what I'm experimenting with, I already saw enough. | |

There’s a missing “vibes” or “taste” benchmark I think. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [eckr](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=eckr) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289920) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292035) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290327)\[–\]<br>Maybe this is just my experience, but have people had trouble with 3.6 Flash just... getting things it has seen in its context correct? Or for example (this is something that happened like yesterday) I asked it to compare the uses of A and B in a language I was learning, and the way I typed it was "Please compare how these two are compared differently: A VS B", and then... it proceeded to compare "VS" and "B". I'm not kidding.<br>Personally whenever I use Gemini I've just been using 3.1 Pro because I've had insane trouble with them getting things incorrect like this. Hopefully they'll fix it soon / they've fixed it with 3.7 Flash. | |

Different teams, different hardware stacks. What do we make of this? (1) There must not really be any deep secrets/moats right now? (2) improving models is not something you can throw only intelligence at now? | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [instagraham](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=instagraham) [47 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49295386) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49293799) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292364)\[–\]<br>Funnily enough, I just ran a task on AI Studio with 3.6 yesterday and got 3.7 to do a similar one today; so it serves as an interesting and quick comparisons between the old and the new (usually, if enough time passes between your use of one model and the next, you'll have a sourer view of it than its actual competence suggests).<br>It hallucinated in both cases despite being given an API key and building a lot of pipes to access data using this. It was a simple "oh shit" fix moment for the model, but weird how eager it was to hallucinate despite the process being designed for it to be data-driven.<br>We should move past the idea that benchmarks alone tell us whether a model is getting better. I would've had the same experience a year or two ago with 1.5, and the solution would've been similar (keep prompting). I've been investing time into making system prompts and input prompts more meticulous, but the fundamental "it will make shit up" problem still remains, even though it shouldn't when the job involves calling tools.<br>I know this sounds like I'm expecting superpowers of it (I'm not), but my point is just that these incremental benchmark gains may not reflect user experience. | |

Side-by-side comparisons with the exact same inputs or it didn't happen, that's my rule going forward. Test all the things, believe nothing. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [dudeinhawaii](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=dudeinhawaii) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290004) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49292258) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49288926)\[–\]<br>I want to like Gemini models but my problem thus far has been a lack of coding chops. They still make mistakes, importantly, without correcting them for things like hallucinated API calls or code that doesn't run but they never bothered building or running. I know a lot of this can be fixed with workflows but it still feels like a failing.<br>GPT-5.6 or Claude models haven't delivered to me non-running code in ages.<br>Whenever I have Gemini in the flow, it's fast, but mistake riddled. I have low confidence in the output.<br>I've had some success with Opus driving Gemini models. It's pointless for GPT family since Sol is cheap enough or can drive terra/luna for arguably better performance, same speed, and better outcome.<br>As for all of the talk in this thread about modalities. Every SOTA model takes screenshots and verifies work now. Grok-4.6 does this, Luna does it, etc. They can also all work \_from\_ a screen shot or mockup provided.<br>I don't think it's a major selling point when every model can do it well and reasonably fast.<br>That said, eagerly awaiting "pro" and improvements to antigravity. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [seizethecheese](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=seizethecheese) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289600) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289222) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289425) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289543)\[–\]<br>Think of it this way: you are at an enterprise business. You have a workflow implemented a year ago that is working just fine. Swapping out the model for a new one changes behavior in unpredictable ways. Eventually, you'll do it once cost is low enough, but it takes serious labor to validate this, so you'll wait a long enough time for Google to make money. | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [nickserv](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=nickserv) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289543) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289222) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289425) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289600) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289443)\[–\]<br>Prompts can certainly be tuned to a particular model, where updating the model actually results in worse performance. This is perhaps less true today than a year or two ago, but we have seen this on newer models as well. Typically, the less specific the instructions are, the less it's a problem. But sometimes you really need to get into specifics to get good results. Area of work is code porting and translation. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [mintflow](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=mintflow) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49294066) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289222) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291648)\[–\]<br>Given my codex have limited usage and I get a idea to build a on device clipboard translator with menubar when read a article find some words not seens before<br>So i launched agy and find seems it have a 3.7 flash i thought its latest,until i see this article i know it's just released<br>after serveral rounds prompt(the initial prd prompt is by chatgpt), it use 2.9k user message token and 42.5k reponse tokens with gemini 3.7 flash(low) after i check the status, i got a working on device translator, its cool<br>the model seems also p and retty fast and the generated app looks good and easy to use<br>but agy cli is a bit unintuitve and i also check the guy that release the latest agy, seems the guy does not commit much ? or perhaps agy does not open source and only use github as a issue feedback channel | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [ksajadi](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=ksajadi) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49291648) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294066) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291276)\[–\]<br>i just wish google cloud ux was remotely as good as their models. they made some progress with their studio, but then in a true google fashion, product names keep changing (Gemini, Anti-gravity, Vertex, Google AI,...) as well as confusion and complexity for something as simple as registering agy cli with a Google cloud project.<br>today i wanted to link agy to a google cloud project, for that i had to enable 5 different APIs in google cloud UI, then create a subscription for Gemini Enterprise (whatever that is), then link it to a project, then assign it to a user. and after all that, agy couldn't find the subscription.<br>the best part: i couldn't cancel the subscription. so i just paid $35 for one month and left it. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [pests](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=pests) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293381) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291648) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291276)\[–\]<br>Gemini is the model family.<br>Antigravity is their agentic coding app / IDE. There is two products, one is chat-only the other is more standard IDE.<br>Google AI Studio is a consumer/developer playground with a in-browser IDE meant for prototypes or demos, there is a gallery of demos etc. Easily shared, easy key access.<br>Vertex is the AI offering from the GCP side of the company, that is going to target more enterprise or business solutions (scaling, data governance, security, production deployents, etc) | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [correlator](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=correlator) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293642) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294060) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49300125)\[–\]<br>We run a platform that serves models from many of the frontier players to power conversations and workflows. Gemini flash-2.5 was a game changer for us when it came out. Cheap, fast, and reliable.<br>We are now considering dropping support for the model family all together. All of their models require significant scrubbing of errant thinking blocks, inner monologues, and it's consuming more engineering resources than it's worth. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [yassa9](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=yassa9) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289708) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289487) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289185)\[–\]<br>its essentially the same model being trained continuously 24/7 with the company periodically publishing just a new checkpoint<br>each new checkpoint can benefit from better reasoning training, RL on specific tasks and more synthetic data<br>So why do they seem to release around the same time ? <br>my guess is because they time major releases around quarterly earnings, investor meetings and other important business milestones. <br>Once one company announces a major update, the others also have an incentive to ship their latest checkpoint rather than look like they r falling behind. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [aix1](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=aix1) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289688) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289321) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49296313)\[–\]<br>The "let's make money by selling/renting out TPUs" faction has won and the "let's make money by training and selling a frontier model" faction has lost.<br>And it's arguably not crazy, at least if SemiAnalysis's estimates are to be believed:<br>```<br>  * 20% of all TPU shipments from Q3 2026 through Q4 2027 are sold to SPVs serving Anthropic ($150B of contracted revenue); vs<br>  * ~$12B ARR for Gemini.<br>```<br>[https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/gemini-is-cooked-but-g...](https://newsletter.semianalysis.com/p/gemini-is-cooked-but-gcp-is-cooking) <br>Because they compete for the same scarce resource, the result is a resource crunch for the group that's lost: [https://www.latimes.com/business/story/2026-05-18/inside-ai-...](https://www.latimes.com/business/story/2026-05-18/inside-ai-compute-crunch-driving-google-researchers-to-quit) | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [deadmutex](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=deadmutex) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290088) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289321) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289688) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49296313)\[–\]<br>\> The "let's make money by selling/renting out TPUs" faction has won and the "let's make money by training and selling a frontier model" faction has lost.<br>Citation needed.<br>also, why can't a massive company do two things? | |
| |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [jerf](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=jerf) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290806) \| [root](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49289321) \| [parent](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290088) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49290177)\[–\]<br>"why can't a massive company do two things?"<br>It's a variation of opportunity cost. A company that has an opportunity to take $1 and make $1.50 on it can't justify an opportunity to spend $1 and make $1.25, even though a less profitable company may make a good living on that. When considering capital allocation, Google has to consider the opportunity cost of investing more into their highly lucrative ads business. Another company that has no access to such a lucrative business uses different opportunity cost when it comes to allocating capital. It can easily be the case that Google could end up justify being in the business of renting out shovels and end up chased out of the business of using the shovels to create AIs entirely because that turns out not to be where the money is. I'm not saying that's obviously inevitable; I'm saying it's a possible and reasonable outcome.<br>That's why even though the industry produces giants, these giants can never just eat everything. Even though it seems like they have all the money, it isn't practical for them to try to do everything and in fact limits get hit very quickly for anything other than the primary, lucrative business.<br>Apparently there is no snappy term for this in the business space, according to such AI searches as I have run. | |

One counter to this is markowitz portfolio theory, or "diversification effect". | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [IFC\_LLC](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=IFC_LLC) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49290035) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49294272) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49302299)\[–\]<br>Like, I understand everything, but by this time I don't give anything about any of those announcements.<br>Theoretically there is some difference between Fable and Opus or Grok and GPT, but at the end of the day I'd look at the bottom left of my screen and to my amusement find out that for the past 3-4 hours I've been using model \_\_\_\_\_\_.<br>If the results are semi-decent, I'd keep it on, if not - I'd randomly switch the model and try again.<br>Actual thing that would affect my selection would be a number of unused tokens I have left for a model \_\_\_\_ for this week.<br>Maybe it's cause I'm using those for programming and log parsing and all of them are decent enough, but other than that - there are no leaps I see. | |

| ![](https://news.ycombinator.com/s.gif) |  | [goochgibbler](https://news.ycombinator.com/user?id=goochgibbler) [48 days ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49293864) \| [prev](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49296674) \| [next](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49289112#49291083)\[–\]<br>I started vibe coding with Gemini. At first very exciting, we got a rough program up quickly. And then, code corruption. Over and over. I started to document every single file, every single step, writing explicit rules to not fake data and create for real world use, but everyday I kept catching Gemini errors, which turned into flat out lies. Generating fake test data instead of pulling real data. Making up test answers. Saying features were implemented that weren't. It even coded fake python files that printed made up results. I think it realized I wasn't doing code reviews. Every day was spent chasing defects and rolling back. After Gemini admitted to faking 7 tests i let Claude review the code, and it fixed it almost immediately asking why half the features were broken or missing. Well Claude, because Gemini flash did the least amount of work to make me happy. Impressive. Very human. Very frustrating. | |
